The Relational Machine Calculus
Chris Barrett, Daniel Castle, Willem Heijltjes

Abstract
. This paper presents the Relational Machine Calculus (RMC): a simple, foundational model of first-order relational programming. The RMC originates from the Functional Machine Calculus (FMC), which generalizes the lambda-calculus and its standard call-by-name stack machine in two directions. One, "locations", introduces multiple stacks, which enable effect operators to be encoded into the abstraction and application constructs. The second, "sequencing", introduces the imperative notions of "skip" and "sequence", similar to kappa-calculus and concatenative programming languages. The key observation of the RMC is that the first-order fragment of the FMC exhibits a latent duality which, given a simple decomposition of the relevant constructors, can be concretely expressed as an involution on syntax. Semantically, this gives rise to a sound and complete calculus for string diagrams of Frobenius monoids. We consider unification as the corresponding symmetric generalization of beta-reduction. By further including standard operators of Kleene algebra, the RMC embeds a range of computational models: the kappa-calculus, logic programming, automata, Interaction Nets, and Petri Nets, among others. These embeddings preserve operational semantics, which for the RMC is again given by a generalization of the standard stack machine for the lambda-calculus. The equational theory of the RMC (which supports reasoning about its operational semantics) is conservative over both the first-order lambda-calculus and Kleene algebra, and can be oriented to give a confluent reduction relation.

1.
Introduction

The
\lambda
-calculus is widely considered the canonical model of functional programming. However, there is no similarly agreed foundation of
relational
programming. Paradigmatic examples of relational programming are logic programming, based on unification
(
1
,
58
,
39
,
72
,
43
)
, and database query languages, based on Tarski’s relational algebra
(
22
,
75
)
.
Relations also serve as the (intended) semantics for a wide range of languages. For example, non-deterministic finite automata, Kleene algebra and its variants — some of which subsume propositional Hoare logic
(
25
,
50
)
— and monadic languages for non-determinism
(
60
)
.
Furthermore, the category of relations is a simple folk model of (differential) linear logic and thus of the linear
\lambda
-calculus; via the Kleisli construction, it is also the basis of a simple quantitative model of the plain
\lambda
-calculus
(
29
,
37
,
53
)
, which can even be seen as underlying game semantics and intersection types
(
35
,
26
,
63
,
16
)
.

Here we present a foundational model of (first-order, sequential) relational programming: the
Relational Machine Calculus (RMC)
.
We first set out the aims and constraints that informed its design. Then, in the remainder of the introduction, we detail the origin of the RMC: from a first-order
\lambda
-calculus through several adaptations to incorporate the relational paradigm, including
duality
,
unification
, and
non-determinism
. The body of the paper is dedicated to justifying our claim of meeting the design criteria we now set out.

1.1.
Design criteria

The space of all possible (first-order, sequential) relational models of computation is bewildering, and indeed there is a proliferation of languages in this space, indicating its importance and range of applications. Yet, we find none in the literature which satisfy the following — minimal, but stringent — design criteria that we would expect to be met by a "
\lambda
-calculus of relational computation".

Denotational semantics::

has a relational and categorical semantics, through quotienting by a local equational theory;

Duality::

exhibits a syntactic involution which switches input and output, and denotes the relational converse;

Operational semantics::

preserves that of standard models of first-order, sequential (relational) computation;

Confluence::

orienting the equational theory gives a confluent reduction relation, sound for the operational semantics.

We proceed to motivate these criteria, which serve to tame the design space and establish standards for success in our programme.

Denotational semantics

A minimal qualifying criterion for any relational calculus is to have a denotational semantics in the category of sets and relations. We further expect a categorical semantics in an appropriate symmetric monoidal category which abstracts the structural properties of relations; we shall see momentarily our preferred candidate.

Duality

While the category of sets and relations embeds that of sets and functions, it exhibits very different structural properties; mainly, a perfect duality between input and output given by relational converse. We consider this duality a defining feature of relational computation. A paradigmatic example is the following Prolog program to concatenate two lists.

concat([],L,L)
concat([E | L1], L2, [E | L3]) :- concat(L1,L2,L3)
Fixing input lists
L1
and
L2
in the relation
concat(L1,L2,L3)
returns their concatenation as
L3
, but by fixing the value
L3
instead, the relation may be run in reverse to (non-deterministically) return every way of splitting
L3
into
L1
and
L2
.

Duality is a common theme in programming language research
(
70
,
31
,
36
)
, and in mathematics and physics more generally. Its aesthetic appeal barely needs justification; practically, it offers parsimony of expression by identifying a dual program (theorem) with each program (theorem) written. We require our language to feature duality in a direct and natural way: by a
syntactic
involution.

Operational semantics

We expect our language to be expressive enough to encode a range of relational models of first-order, sequential computation, including the first-order
\lambda
-calculus, automata, logic programming, and Petri nets. However, any sufficiently expressive language can encode any other: we would like the embeddings into our calculus to be, in some way,
natural
. As our criterion for this, we specify that operational semantics is preserved, for models where it is defined. This means that (at least, with respect to
operational
semantics — a relatively fine-grained notion) the encoded models may be viewed as
fragments
of our language, built from its more fundamental primitives.

Confluence

The intended relational semantics will impose an equational theory on our calculus. Following the central notion of
\beta
-reduction in the
\lambda
-calculus, we expect the equational theory (modulo certain congruences) to be orientable as a reduction relation, and that this relation is
confluent
, contributing in an essential way to an effective solution to this theory.

1.2.
First-order lambda-calculus and duality

Our point of departure is the first-order
\lambda
-calculus, as first appeared as the
\kappa
-calculus
(
40
,
67
)
and recently as the first-order fragment of the Functional Machine Calculus
(
42
,
4
)
(FMC). These calculi feature three of our desired properties: a
denotational semantics
(albeit in Cartesian categories), an
operational semantics
in a simple stack machine, and
confluent reduction
. Crucially, they also exhibit the potential for
duality
; and in the formulation of the FMC, this is directly observable in the syntax.

Taking the operational perspective, the first-order FMC is an instruction language for a simple stack machine. Terms are sequences of
push

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
operations over variables
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
or constant values
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, separated by sequential composition
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
. The following example shows a term and its associated string diagram; the type is given by the size of its input and output stacks.

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{28.21292pt}{22.39616pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 39.04 30.99)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,\langle z\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,[z]\,{;}\,[y]~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}3{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}3\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Operationally, a term evaluates from left to right;
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pops the head off the stack, say a constant
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and substitutes
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in the remaining computation;
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pushes the value substituted for
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
onto the stack. We write our stacks with the head to the right, to match the order of
pushes
; our term then takes a stack
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}e\,d\,c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\,e\,d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle y\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle z\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}e\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

In this way, our term implements a relation between input and output stacks, as illustrated by the string diagram, where the wires represent the manipulation of the items on the stack (shown with the head at the top). The simple typing discipline of giving input and output arity tames the asymmetric nature of stacks, resulting in an internal language for symmetric monoidal categories (SMCs) in the linear case (where each
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is matched by a unique
push

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to its right, and vice versa).

In the FMC, a
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
the remaining computation, is a first-order lambda-abstraction, which we write
\kappa x.M
following the
\kappa
-calculus. A
push

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
corresponds to an application
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, restricted to first order by forcing the argument to be a variable. The stack machine is then a simplified Krivine machine
(
51
)
, replacing environments with substitution. This highlights a key aspect. A
\kappa
-abstraction is a
binder
, making the variable
x

local
to
\kappa x.M
. However,
binding
and
variable scope
are the main notions that will need to be re-visited in light of duality. A pop
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is therefore not a binder, and variables in this fragment of the RMC are
global
. This does not prevent us from encoding first-order
\lambda
-calculus: in the first-order setting, terms are never duplicated, removing any issues with variable capture or
\alpha
-conversion. We may then simulate binding by stipulating that each
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
has a unique variable, which only occurs in
pushes
to its right (this is
Barendregt’s convention
). Later, when duplication of terms returns, we will re-introduce local variable scope with a binding
new variable
construct
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, justifying the core fragment of the RMC as a decomposition of abstraction into its two distinct roles:
pop
and
new
.

Duality

Our formulation of first-order
\lambda
-calculus is tailored to reveal its latent duality, effected simply by reversing a term while switching
push
and
pop
. The dual of our previous example term:

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,\langle z\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[z]\,{;}\,[y]\,{;}\,[x]~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}3{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}3\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~.

The linear fragment, where terms represent
permutations
, is closed under duality. By contrast, the
non-linear
first-order
\lambda
-calculus is characterised by
duplication
and
deletion
, embodied by the
diagonal
and
terminal
below left. These yield the dual terms below right, which we label
matching
and
arbitrary
, that feature multiple related
pops
, or zero.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Semantics tells us that the new terms should represent the relational converse of the diagonal and terminal functions, given respectively by the partial function that sends
(x,x)
to
x
and is otherwise undefined, and the non-deterministic function sending the trivial input to all possible outputs.

Operationally, the required behaviour of
matching

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for an input stack of two identical values,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, is to return
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and for distinct values,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, is to fail. We achieve this by generalising
pop
to hold also
constants
,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, interpreted as an
assertion
that the head of the stack is
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, with failure for any other constant. Then
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
evaluates by first popping
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, substituting it for
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in both
pop
and
push
to get
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle c\rangle\,{;}\,[c]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, which then pops and replaces the second value
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
(or fails for
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
).

The second term,
arbitrary

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, illustrates that stack values must include variables, and hence must be substituted for: the term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for a stack
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
incurs a substitution of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for the second
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The behaviour of
push
and
pop
thus becomes perfectly symmetric, with substitutions in both the stack and the remaining term.

Altogether these relations are abstracted categorically as
Frobenius monoids
, which we take to capture non-linear computation in the presence of duality, given in string diagrams as above and satisfied by the
Frobenius equation
below.

=
=

Intuitively, this implements the idea that
connectivity = identity
: connected wires represent a single value. In our calculus, this is modeled by
variable names
; and indeed the three diagrams in the above equation may all be represented by the term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Frobenius monoids have received a huge amount of interest over the last decade or so, since they were identified as a fundamental primitive of quantum computation by the ZX-calculus
(
23
,
24
,
47
)
. Since then, they have been adopted as primitives in a wide range of string diagrammatic languages, including those capturing conjunctive queries, relational algebra, and aspects of logic programming

(
13
,
6
,
38
,
10
)
. In programming language theory specifically, they have received significantly less attention, but have been used in a synthetic axiomatization of the operation of
exact conditioning
in probabilistic programming
(
27
,
73
)
and to model the (partial) inverse of duplication in reversible languages
(
48
)
. The RMC offers a novel
operational
account of Frobenius monoids, thus filling a gap in the literature and bridging the study of string-diagrammatic and programming languages.

1.3.
The full calculus

The calculus thus far is generated by:
push

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
operations,
sequential composition

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and its unit
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, the imperative
skip
. Associativity of composition will be implemented via the machine and the equational theory. To reach our desired expressivity, we extend this with a careful selection of features, outlined below.

Algebraic terms and unification

The
\beta
-reduction relation is the interaction of a consecutive
push
and
pop
, say
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[y]\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, to incur a (global) substitution
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{y/x\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. With constants, both
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[c]\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\,{;}\,\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
incur
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{c/x\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, while
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[c]\,{;}\,\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
succeeds
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[d]\,{;}\,\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
fails.

Viewing a redex as a formal equation, these are the familiar rules of
first-order unification
, restricted to variables and constants. We generalize our calculus to allow
algebraic terms
1
1

1

We write ”algebraic terms” to avoid name clashing with ”first-order terms”, which here refer to terms of the RMC, in contrast with ”higher-order terms” of the
\lambda
-calculus.
as values, and will implement unification on the machine and through a symmetric version of
\beta
-reduction.
Evaluating a redex
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\,{;}\,\langle s\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
will thus produce substitutions constituting the most general unifier of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}s\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, or fail if none exists.

Kleene Algebra

To internalize the partial and non-deterministic semantics of relations, we introduce non-deterministic
sum

({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and
failure

({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
, its unit. Indeed, the presence of non-determinism is often considered a defining feature of relational programming. With sequencing already present, we also desire a Kleene star construction
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}-^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
to model infinitary behaviour,
e.g.
, of logic programming. Our full calculus will thus be conservative over Kleene algebra (KA).

The atoms of KA may be interpreted as programs, with the axioms of KA acting as a set of (weak) fundamental laws any standard non-deterministic, sequential language should satisfy
(
45
,
46
,
50
)
. However, an
operational
interpretation of KA is typically only given via its correspondence with finite state automata. Our calculus can be viewed as a modification of KA to include the dual primitives of
push
and
pop
, replacing the atoms of KA and supporting its extension with a
direct
operational semantics.

Local variables

Evaluation for Kleene star, taking a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
non-deterministically to a sequence
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,\dots\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
of any length, re-introduces duplication of terms into the calculus, bringing with it the familiar problems of variable identity and
\alpha
-conversion. We adopt a standard solution: making the scope of a variable explicit with a
new variable
construct,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, familiar variously from nominal Kleene algebra
(
34
)
, functional logic programming
(
39
)
,
\pi
-calculus
(
59
)
, and the
\nu
-calculus
(
66
)
as a fragment of ML. This restores locality and gives a proper decomposition of
\kappa
-abstraction into its two roles: first, as the binder of new variables, and second, as an instruction to
pop
from the stack.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\kappa x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Locations

We inherit a further feature from the Functional Machine Calculus: multiple stacks (or streams) on the abstract machine, indexed in a set of
locations
. This captures several computational effects: mutable store, as stacks restricted to depth one; input and output, as dedicated streams; and a probabilistic generator, as a stream of random bits — remarkably,
while retaining confluent reduction

(
42
)
. The required generalization of the syntax is a simple parameterization of
push
and
pop
in a location
a
, as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
respectively, to operate on the indicated stack. Thereby, the RMC subsumes the operational semantics of the given effects, while
\beta\eta
-equivalence captures their algebraic theory
(
42
,
4
)
. This extension allows the simple modelling of stateful models of computation such as Turing machines and Petri nets.

2.
The Relational Machine Calculus

We define the
Relational Machine Calculus
(RMC) as follows. We assume a countable set of
locations

{A}=\{a,b,c\dots\}
, which each represent a stack on the abstract machine. Stacks hold algebraic terms over a signature
\Sigma
of function symbols
f^{n}
of arity
n
.

Definition 2.1 (Relational Machine Calculus).

Values

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}s{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
(computation) terms

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are given by the grammars below.

\begin{array}[]{@{}rrll@{}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}s{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\Coloneqq&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f^{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}(t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8},\dots,t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f^{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in\Sigma)&\text{algebraic terms}\\
\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\Coloneqq&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\text{Kleene algebra}\\[5.0pt]
&\mid&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\text{stack operations}\\[5.0pt]
&\mid&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\text{variable scope}\end{array}

From left to right, the computation terms are
skip
or
nil

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
(sequential) composition

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
Kleene star

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
zero
or
failure

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, a
sum
of terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, a
push

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
of the value
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to the location
a
, a
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
from the location
a
to unify with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and a
new
variable introduction
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
which binds
x
in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Operator precedence: Kleene star binds tightest, then sequencing, then
new

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and finally sum; then
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N+P\,{;}\,Q^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(M\,{;}\,N))+(P\,{;}\,(Q^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}))\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. We often omit the superscript on
f^{n}
.

The calculus exhibits
duality
as a syntactic involution.

Definition 2.2 (Duality).

Define
duality

(-)^{\dagger}
on terms as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}\\
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0})^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcl}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})^{\dagger}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}\end{array}

We shall see throughout the paper how various operational, equational, and semantic notions either dualize or respect duality.

2.1.
Operational Semantics

The small-step operational semantics is given by a stack machine, the
relational abstract machine
or
relational machine
.

Definition 2.3 (Relational Machine).

A
state
is a triple
({S_{A}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},K)
of: a
memory

S_{A}
, a family of stacks indexed in a set of locations
A
; a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
; and a
continuation stack

K
. These are defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rrll}K,L&\Coloneqq&\varepsilon~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K&\text{continuation stacks}\\[5.0pt]
S,T&\Coloneqq&\varepsilon~\mid~S\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\text{operand stacks}\\[5.0pt]
S_{A}&\Coloneqq&\{S_{a}\mid a\in A\}&\text{memories}\end{array}

The addition of a stack
S_{a}
to a memory
S_{A}
(
a\notin A
) is written
S_{A}\cdot S_{a}
. We abbreviate
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8},\dots,t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(T)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
where
T={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\dots t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]a\,{;}\,\dots\,{;}\,[t_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[T]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,\dots\,{;}\,a\langle t_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle T\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
(note the inversion). The
transitions
of the machine are given in Figure
1
, read top–to–bottom, and are non-deterministic: a state transitions to a formal sum of states, represented by branching of the transitions. A
run
of the machine is a single rooted path in the machine tree, not necessarily to a leaf, shown with a double line as below.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&L\end{array}

A run is
successful
if it terminates in a state
(\,T_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,\varepsilon\,)
. A state where no transition rules apply represents
failure
, and is considered to have
zero
branches.

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}cc@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\\
\hline\cr(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\quad(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\\
\hline\cr(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\quad(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}\cdot S_{a}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{y/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}~(y~\text{fresh})\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}\cdot S_{a}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(R)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle f(T)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,R&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle T\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}(S_{A}\cdot S_{a})&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}K\end{array}~({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}(S_{A}\cdot S_{a})&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}K\end{array}~({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\end{array}

Figure 1.
Transitions of the Relational Machine
Transitions of the Relational Machine

Machine evaluation of a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for an input memory
S_{A}
gives a (possibly infinite) number of successful runs, each with a return memory
T_{A}
. The big-step evaluation function
(S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
will collect these as a multiset. Since variables are global and machine steps substitute into the continuation stack, evaluation returns also a finite substitution map
\sigma
with each return memory
T_{A}
, as a pair
(T_{A},\sigma)
. In a composition
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, the subsitutions from
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
can then be applied to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Notation

We denote the empty multiset by
[]
, a singleton by
[(T_{A},\sigma)]
, and multiset union by
\sqcup
. Substitution maps
\sigma
and
\tau
are applied to a term as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\sigma M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and composed as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\sigma\tau)M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\sigma(\tau M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The empty map is
\varepsilon
, and
\sigma\setminus{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is as
\sigma
except undefined on
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The
n
-fold composition of a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{0}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{n+1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,M^{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Definition 2.4 (Big-step operational semantics).

The big-step operational semantics of the RMC is given by the evaluation function
(-\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
below, where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is globally
fresh
in the
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
case.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\,\Downarrow\,}l@{~=~}l@{\qquad}r@{}}{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[(S_{A},\varepsilon)]\\
{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\lx@intercol\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}l@{}}[\,(U_{A},\tau\sigma)\mid&(T_{A},\sigma)\in S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\\
&(U_{A},\tau)\in T_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\sigma N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,]\end{array}\hfil\\
{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,(S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\\
{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&(S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\sqcup(S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\\
{S_{A}\cdot S_{a}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[(S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\varepsilon)]\\
{S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[(S_{A}\cdot S_{a},\varepsilon)]\\
{S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[(\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}(S_{A}\cdot S_{a}),\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\})]&({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\\
{S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[(\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}(S_{A}\cdot S_{a}),\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}/{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\})]&({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\\
{S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(R)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle f(T)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&S_{A}\cdot S_{a}\,R\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle T\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\lx@intercol[\,(T_{A},\sigma\setminus{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\mid(T_{A},\sigma)\in S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{y/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,]\hfil\\
{S_{A}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&[]&\text{(otherwise)}\end{array}

The function
\lfloor-\rfloor
takes a multiset to its underlying set, and the function
\mathsf{ret}(-)
projects onto only
return memories
in
\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor
.

We assume that fresh variables are generated globally, without clashing. In particular, in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M)\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
the variable instantiating the
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
must be free for
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as well as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. One may address this practically by carrying along the
formal parameters
of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M)\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
into
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
; we omit this for brevity.

Proposition 2.5 (Big-step semantics is well defined).

The evaluation function
(-\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
is a total function.

Proposition 2.6 (Small-step and big-step semantics agree).

For every memory
S_{A}
, term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and continuation
K
, there is a bijection between the elements
(T_{A},\sigma)
of
(S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and successful runs

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\sigma K\end{array}~.

3.
Encoding computational models, I

We embed a number of computational models (or algorithms, in the case of unification) into the RMC to illustrate the origins and purposes of its various constructs. Observe, in particular, that operational semantics is preserved, often in a strong sense. We use only one, unnamed stack, writing
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Encodings of stateful languages, making use of multiple locations, are given in Section
8
.

Notation

We introduce vector notation for stacks of variables
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\ldots x_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and emphasize the reversal in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,\ldots\,{;}\,\langle x_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
by pointing the arrow left. Concatenation is given by juxtaposition. We further abbreviate
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\ldots\exists x_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

3.1.
Regular expressions

Regular expressions (REs) over an alphabet
\Sigma
of constants
a,b,c,\dots
are given by the following grammar, with their embedding
\llbracket-\rrbracket
into RMC-terms below it, given by juxtaposition.

\begin{array}[]{r@{~~\Coloneqq~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c}E,E^{\prime}&\varepsilon&E\,E^{\prime}&E^{*}&\varnothing&(E|E^{\prime})&~c\in\Sigma\\[5.0pt]
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[c]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Here, constant values
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are
pushed
to the stack. The regular language
\mathcal{L}_{E}
defined by
E
— a set of words (stacks) over
\Sigma
— is thus given directly by the evaluation of
\llbracket E\rrbracket
.

Proposition 3.1 (Regular expressions embed).

For an RE
E
,

\mathcal{L}_{E}~=~\mathsf{ret}\lfloor\varepsilon\,{\Downarrow}\,{\llbracket E\rrbracket}\rfloor~.

There is a
dual
embedding
\llbracket-\rrbracket^{\dagger}
of REs into the grammar below, given by composition of the original embedding with duality.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle c\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Here, constant values
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are
popped
from the stack. The embedding can be considered as defining a computation which
tests
words (stacks), with a word
S
accepted if
\varepsilon\in\mathsf{ret}\lfloor S\,{\Downarrow}\,{\llbracket E\rrbracket^{\dagger}}\rfloor
. The characteristic (or indicator) function of a regular language
\mathcal{L}_{E}
is thus given directly by evaluation of
\llbracket E\rrbracket^{\dagger}
.

3.2.
Unification

A (first-order) unification algorithm
(
56
)
takes a set of formal equations
E=\{{s_{1}}\doteq{t_{1}},\dots,{s_{n}}\doteq{t_{n}}\}
and returns a most general unifier (MGU), a minimal subsitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma{s_{i}}=\sigma{t_{i}}
for all
i
, if one exists. In the RMC, we may encode such equations as redexes:
\llbracket s\doteq t\rrbracket={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[s]\,{;}\,\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Unification (as a general concept) can then be viewed as embedding in the RMC as the following fragment.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Terms are sequences of pushes and pops of first-order values to and from this location. A set of equations
E
is encoded as follows.

\llbracket\{s_{1}\doteq t_{1},\dots,s_{n}\doteq t_{n}\}\rrbracket={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[s_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,\dots\,{;}\,[s_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,\langle t_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,\dots\,{;}\,\langle t_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Note that we have chosen nested redexes, since that is also what the machine reduction for function symbols produces; but a sequence of redexes would have been equally valid. We then observe that evaluation returns an MGU if one exists.

Proposition 3.2 (First-order unification embeds).

For a set of formal equations over algebraic terms
E
we have the following, where
\sigma
is an MGU for
E
, if one exists, and otherwise
\varepsilon\,{\Downarrow}\,{\llbracket E\rrbracket}=[]
.

\varepsilon\,{\Downarrow}\,{\llbracket E\rrbracket}=[(\varepsilon,\sigma)]

For machine evaluation, which does not return a substitution explicitly, we may obtain
\sigma
by populating the initial stack with the free variables
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\dots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in
E
. This is the domain of
\sigma
; the machine then substitutes into each variable
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to return
\sigma{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, as follows, so that the relation from input to output stack captures the MGU
\sigma
.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\dots~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llbracket E\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rrbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}\sigma{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\dots~\sigma{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\end{array}

3.3.
Kappa-calculus

Hasegawa’s
\kappa
-calculus
(
40
)
, which featured in the introduction, as formulated by Power and Thielecke
(
67
)
, embeds as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{~~\Coloneqq~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c@{~~\mid~~}c}E,E^{\prime}&~E;E^{\prime}&\mathsf{PUSH}\,x&\kappa x.E\\[5.0pt]
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

A
\kappa
-abstraction (first-order
\lambda
-abstraction) decomposes into a
new

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and a
pop

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, so that
\kappa x
acts as a binder as well as taking input. The asymmetry of this construct, which rules out terms such as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, gives the Cartesian semantics. The machine behaviour illustrates that the decomposition gives the correct behaviour: pop and substitute.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,\{y/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr S\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle y\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\{y/x\}M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K\\
\hline\cr S&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\{z/x\}M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K\\
\hline\cr S&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{z/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\end{array}

Note that Power and Thielecke’s formulation of the
\kappa
-calculus, above, unfortunately does not include the unit for sequencing; an omission which is fixed in the FMC and RMC.

3.4.
Pattern-matching

In a
\lambda
-calculus with
pattern-matching

(
14
,
20
,
49
)
, abstractions are over
patterns
, which generally are algebraic terms, instead of over variables. Since we do not have higher-order abstraction, we will stay within
\kappa
-calculus. We will use
pairing
as our only pattern, as is standard in a Cartesian setting.

E,E^{\prime}~\Coloneqq~E;E^{\prime}~\mid~\mathsf{PUSH}~p~\mid~\kappa p.E\qquad p,q~\Coloneqq~x~\mid~(p,q)

In
\kappa p.E
, the free variables of the pattern
p
bind in
E
. The embedding in the RMC captures this by introducing each variable as
new
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[p]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle p\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\mathsf{fv}(p))\end{array}

The behaviour of the RMC-encoding is slightly more general than that of the original pattern-matching calculus, since it can deal with situations such as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\,{;}\,\exists y.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists z.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle(y,z)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
(by substituting into
x
), which the latter cannot. Pattern-matching calculi address this by introducing explicit product types. With that constraint, the overall behaviour agrees, as expected.

Languages using
let-bindings
with patterns, used for string diagrams or monoidal categories
(
41
,
67
)
, admit an analogous encoding to that of monadic let-bindings in the FMC
(
42
)
, as follows.

\mathsf{let}~E=p~\mathsf{in}~E^{\prime}\quad\mapsto\quad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}E\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle p\rangle\,{;}\,E{}^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\mathsf{fv}(p))

3.5.
Symmetric pattern-matching

We embed a first-order fragment of
Theseus
, a
reversible
programming language which is both forwards and backwards deterministic
(
69
,
19
,
17
)
. Its
values
are generated from pairing and injections. Computation is performed by application of
isomorphisms
, expressed using
symmetric pattern-matching
syntax below, where the set of values
v_{i}
(respectively,
w_{i}
) are restricted by the type system to be
exhaustive
and
non-overlapping
, guaranteeing reversibility. We omit presentation of the type system here for brevity.

\{~v_{1}\leftrightarrow w_{1}~\mid~\ldots~\mid~v_{n}\leftrightarrow w_{n}~\}

We interpret values using corresponding function symbols, and embed isomorphisms as below.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}{}_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle v_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,[w_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]~+~\ldots~+~\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}{}_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle v_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,[w_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Each
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}{}_{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
contains exactly the variables of
{v_{i}}
and
{w_{i}}
. Isomorphism
inversion
is given by
(-)^{\dagger}
. The application of isomorphism
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to value
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}v\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is encoded as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[v]\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

3.6.
Prolog

We embed pure Prolog, as defined by the following grammar.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}l@{\quad}l@{\quad}r@{~}l@{}}\text{Terms:}&t\coloneqq&x\mid f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})&\text{Atoms:}&A\coloneqq&P(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\\
\text{Clauses:}&C\coloneqq&A~{:}{-}~A_{1}\dots A_{n}&\text{Programs:}&L\coloneqq&C_{1}\dots C_{n}\end{array}

Prolog by defualt evaluates using a
top-down
evaluation strategy, which takes the query — that is, a chosen atom — as a
goal
to be proved and non-deterministically applies clauses, decomposing the set of goals into sub-goals, succeeding when the set is empty.

We consider atoms, as well as terms, to be modelled in the RMC by algebraic terms.
The embedding of a clause
C
with free variables
\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}
is then given as follows.

{\llbracket A~{:}{-}~A_{1}\dots A_{n}\rrbracket}~=~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle A\rangle\,{;}\,[A_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,\dots\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,[A_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

The stack will hold the set of goals, with the non-deterministic sum of clauses modelling the program itself. To select the correct solution among the non-deterministic outputs, we add a new constant
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\mathsf{END}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to the input stack, which can only be removed when the computation is successfully completed.
The embedding of a query-program pair is thus given below.

\llbracket(Q,C_{1}\ldots C_{n})\rrbracket={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\mathsf{END}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,[Q]\,{;}\,\,(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\ldots+~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\llbracket C_{n}\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0})^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\langle\mathsf{END}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

As we did with unification, by populating the initial stack with the query
Q
we collect the substitutions generated by the machine run. The completed computation then returns the same instantiation of
Q
as does Prolog.

Proposition 3.3 (Prolog embeds).

Given as input a query
Q
and program
L
, the Prolog abstract interpreter
(
74
)
outputs the instance
Q^{\prime}
if and only if

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}Q\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llbracket(Q,L)\rrbracket}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}Q^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\end{array}

In contrast to top-down evaluation,
bottom-up
evaluation starts from the set of
facts
(
i.e.
, clauses with a head
A
but no body
A_{i}
) and non-deterministically applies rules
in reverse
, building up the set of logical consequences of facts, succeeding when the query is reached.
Remarkably, the RMC reveals a syntactic duality between the two strategies: the dual embedding
{\llbracket(Q,L)\rrbracket^{\dagger}}
evaluates the query-program pair via the bottom-up strategy.

Further, although beyond the scope of the current work, we believe that
semi-naive evaluation
— which can asymptotically improve the performance of bottom-up queries — can be formalized in our language as a source-to-source translation.

4.
Equational Theory and Reduction

In this section, we define the equational theory of the RMC and show that it is sound with respect to a natural notion of observational equivalence induced by the operational semantics. We then recover a reduction relation through a natural orientation of (an appropriate subset of) the equations, which we prove confluent. In the star-free fragment, this relation is strongly normalising, giving rise to normal forms.

4.1.
The Equational Theory

The equational theory we define is conservative over Kleene algebra, but contains the following additional axioms. For the
new
variable construct, there are axioms reminiscent of those from
nominal
Kleene algebra
(
34
)
. There are then axioms internalizing unification, with each corresponding to a step in the standard Martelli and Montanari algorithm for unification (including its "occurs check")
(
56
)
. In particular, these axioms include a symmetric version of the
\beta
-law of the
\kappa
-calculus. There is also a weaker, inequational version of the
\eta
-law; and we shall see later that, in the typed setting, we recover the stronger, standard
\eta
-law. Finally, there is an axiom allowing the permutation of locations, familiar from the Functional Machine Calculus
(
42
)
. Observe that the equational theory respects the duality of the calculus, as expected.

Definition 4.1 (Equational theory).

We define the
equational theory

(=)
over terms of the RMC to be the least congruence generated by the following axioms.

•

Those of Kleene algebra: that is,
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
forms an idempotent semiring
2
2

2

That is,
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
is a monoid and
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
is an idempotent commutative monoid, such that
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
distributes over
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is annihilative.

together with:

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\,+\,M\,{;}\,M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\,+\,M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

where
\leq
is the natural partial order:
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
iff
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
In the sequel, we work modulo associativity wherever possible.

•

Those axiomatizing the
new
variable constructor:

\begin{array}[]{l@{~}l@{\quad}l}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=_{\nu}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=_{\nu}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M)\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=_{\nu}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(M+N)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=_{\nu}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M~+~\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists y.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=_{\nu}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists y.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

•

Those axiomatizing
\beta
- and
\eta
-equivalence and unification:

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\,{;}\,[t]a\,{;}\,a\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{t/x\}(N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\not\in{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\,{;}\,[x]a\,{;}\,a\langle t\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{t/x\}(N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\not\in{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\eta}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a[x]\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\upsilon}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[f(S)]a\,{;}\,a\langle f(T)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\upsilon}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[S]a\,{;}\,a\langle T\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[f(S)]a\,{;}\,a\langle g(T)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\upsilon}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle(f\neq g)

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[f(S)]a\,{;}\,a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\omega}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in S)

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]a\,{;}\,a\langle f(S)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle=_{\omega}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in S)

•

Those axiomatizing permutation of locations:

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[s]a\,{;}\,b\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=_{\pi}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\langle t\rangle\,{;}\,[s]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle(a\neq b)

The permutation law suffices to derive the fact that "push" and "pop" on distinct locations commute with each other.

Lemma 4.2 (Permutation).

The following equations are derivable.

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[s]a\,{;}\,[t]b\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]b\,{;}\,[s]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle s\rangle\,{;}\,b\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\langle t\rangle\,{;}\,a\langle s\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad(a\neq b)

Proof.

Derivable from
\pi
together with
\eta,\nu
and
\beta
.
∎

A
\beta
-redex of the
\kappa
-calculus encodes as below, left, assuming a chosen, unnamed location. As such, its
\beta
-law decomposes into the following two axioms (where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is not free in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
).

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]~\,{;}\,~\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=_{\nu}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=_{\beta}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{t/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

The
\beta
-axiom of the RMC generalizes and symmetrizes the second equality above: the
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
construct may additionally be separated from the sub-term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
by some term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, which is also open for substitution.

We now define a notion of observational equivalence of terms, which we call
machine equivalence
, via the big-step operational semantics.
In the following, we take
S_{A}
to be open in general (i.e. it may contain free variables), and possibly sharing variables with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Open memories and substitutions are considered equivalent up to a choice of globally fresh variables. Practically, this means machine runs and memories are equipped with a context; we omit this for brevity.

Definition 4.3 (Machine equivalence).

Machine equivalence
is the relation
(\sim)
on terms defined by

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\text{if}\quad\forall S_{A}.~\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor=\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor~.

Proposition 4.4.

Machine equivalence is a congruence.

We can define a similar
machine refinement

(\lesssim)
relation on terms as follows.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lesssim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\text{if}\quad\forall S_{A}.~\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor\subseteq\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor~

It is easy to see that machine refinement satisfies the property that
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lesssim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
if and only if
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and so
(\lesssim)
stands in the same relation to
(\sim)
as
(\leq)
does to
(=)
in the algebraic theory. In particular, it is a partial order closed under all contexts.

Theorem 4.5 (Soundness of equational theory).

The equational theory is sound with respect to machine equivalence:

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Rightarrow~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~.

4.2.
Reduction and Confluence

(\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

from the equational theory by orientation of the equations.
The
\beta
and
\upsilon
equations have an obvious orientation and the
\nu
equations are oriented to bring
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to the front of a term, or remove it when it does not bind any variables. We do not consider the
\eta
inequation in the untyped case.
To maintain confluence in the presence of the Kleene star, we find it necessary to break the symmetry of the equational theory: we can include either the left-handed
unfolding
reduction
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
