, but not both. We opt, arbitrarily, for the former. We do not find it sensible to consider the conditional equation
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
or its dual as part of reduction.

We consider the main interest of confluence for reduction to be in dealing with the directed
\beta
,
\upsilon
and
\nu
equations. We thus make several further adaptations to the KA fragment of reduction which simplify the confluence proof. We choose to omit the idempotence equation, in keeping with the more general multiset operational semantics; we omit consideration of associativity and commutativity of
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
, although the method of proof should extend straightforwardly to these cases; and we assume right-associativity of sequencing, so terms are of the form
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,(N\,{;}\,\ldots\,{;}\,(P\,{;}\,\star)\ldots)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, which also has the pleasant consequence of eliminating the need to consider
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M)\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{

where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\not\in\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and

.
For this reason,
\beta
-reduction will take place in (also right-associative)
linear contexts
, defined as follows

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}L\{~\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\Coloneqq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{~\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,L\{~\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}L\{~\}\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

and
\upsilon
-reductions will contain an arbitrary post-fixed term. We also enforce a prioritization of left- over right-distributivity, although we expect this can be lifted. Finally, the permutation equation is oriented left–to–right. The reduction relation is overall clearly
sound
(but not complete) with respect to the equational theory, and thus the operational semantics.

Reduction rules of the RMC
Reduction rules of the RMC

Our proof of confluence makes use of techniques from the field of first-order term rewriting by treating the atomic terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as constants and binders
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as distinct function symbols for each
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Local confluence is shown by analysis of critical pairs.

is locally confluent.

We now observe the star-free fragment is strongly normalising (by the measure given in the proof of Proposition
2.5
) and hence confluent by Newman’s Lemma
(
62
)
. Confluence of the full reduction relation then follows from an application of the Hindley-Rosen Lemma
(
44
)
.

In the (strongly normalising) star-free fragment, we thus recover the existence of
normal forms
.
Let
(\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{

be the reflexive, transitive closure of

{}{{}}{}

.
We state the result for the case of one location, but in general
push
and
pop
actions on distinct locations can be arranged according to a chosen ordering.

Corollary 4.9 (Normal forms).

For any closed, star-free RMC-term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, we have that
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.75 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.92 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{*}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, where

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\equiv{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\ldots+N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~,

where each
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{i}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is sum-free and of the form

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{i}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\equiv{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle s_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,\ldots\,{;}\,\langle s_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,[t_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,\ldots\,{;}\,[t_{m}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~,

where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are the variables of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}s_{j}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{k}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Note, normal forms are taken modulo associativity and commutativity of
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and permutation of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. We close this section with a theorem about the equational theory (and thus about operational semantics) proved easily using the existence of normal forms.

Theorem 4.10 (Equations for duality).

We have the following:

•

for any closed, star-free term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, we have
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
;

•

if
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is also sum-free then
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

The first statement is familiar as the unique law added to KA in Kleene algebra
with converse

(
15
)
; the second statement is familiar as the weakening of invertibility in the definition of
inverse monoids
and
inverse cateogries

(
21
)
.

5.
The Simply-Typed RMC

In this section, we introduce a simple typing discipline giving the input and output arity of terms, making their stack use explicit. This results in a natural language for string diagrams where types represent the input and output wires, which we explicate in the subsequent section on categorical semantics.

In a first-order setting, the power of types is naturally limited. Types do not confer termination: the fragment without Kleene star is inherently terminating, while the full RMC is non-terminating also when typed. Nor do types prevent failure, as in the term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
or failure of unification, and this is semantically correct: failure represents the empty relation, and so should not be excluded from the typed calculus.

What types do enforce is a notion of
progress
: they guarantee that there are sufficient elements on the stack for the machine to continue. In particular, they prevent terms with Kleene star from consuming (or producing) an arbitrary number of stack elements. These constraints are essential for correct relational composition, and hence in giving a relational semantics to terms, where types represent the source and target of a relation. Specifically, types allow the sound strengthening the
\eta
-axiom to an equivalence, which is necessary for a monoidal categorical semantics.

Formally, the type of a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
will give the input and output arity
for each location
. With a single, unnamed location, we will have
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for natural numbers
m
and
n
; for a set of locations
A
, we will have an input arity
m_{a}
and output arity
n_{a}
for each
a\in A
.

Definition 5.1 (Simple types).

A
memory type

{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\{n_{a}\mid a\in A\}
is a family of natural numbers in a (finite) set of locations
A
. A
type

{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
consists of an
input
and an
output
memory type.

We may consider a memory type
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as a function from locations to natural numbers, where
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(a)=n_{a}
for
a\in A
and
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(b)=0
for any
b\notin A
, and silently expand
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{B}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for any
B\supset A
. For any natural number
n
we may write
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for the singleton family in
\{a\}
. The
empty
memory type
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
(or simply
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
) is everywhere zero, and the
sum

{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}+m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
of two memory types is given location-wise by
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}(n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}+m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(a)={\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(a)+{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(a)
.

Definition 5.2 (The simply-typed RMC).

The typing rules for simply-typed RMC-terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are given in Figure
3
.

\begin{array}[]{cc}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lx@proof@logical@and{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lx@proof@logical@and{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\

Figure 3.
The simply-typed RMC
The simply-typed RMC

The present type system, which records only the
number
of elements consumed and produced on the stack, is much simplified with respect to that of the FMC
(
42
)
. Being higher-order, the latter records also the
types
of stack elements, as a vector of types
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\kern 0.4pt\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{\tau\kern 0.4pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Here, we only need the length of
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\kern 0.4pt\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{\tau\kern 0.4pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as a natural number
n
; because the RMC is first-order, and also because we have a single-sorted signature
\Sigma
for algebraic terms, by which we assume only a single base type for values. A many-sorted signature, where each sort is represented by a different base type, may be accommodated with stack types
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\kern 0.4pt\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{\tau\kern 0.4pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as on the FMC.

We have the following three basic properties. First,
subject reduction
(reduction preserves types). Second,
expansion
: if the machine runs with a given input stack, it will also run with a larger stack, and the output will be equally larger. Third,
duality
exchanges input and output types.

Lemma 5.3.

The following properties of the type system hold:

•

Subject reduction:

if
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

Duality:

if

To connect types with machine behaviour, we extend types to stacks and memories in the expected way: a stack is typed
S:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
where
n=|S|
, the length of
S
, and a memory is typed
S_{A}:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
where
S_{a}:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for all
a\in A
. Continuation stacks and states are then typed by the following rules.

\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,K:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lx@proof@logical@and{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}K:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\\
(S_{A},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},K):{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\lx@proof@logical@and S_{A}:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}K:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Theorem 5.4 (The machine respects types).

For a run

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&K\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&L\end{array}

if
(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,):{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
then
(\,T_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,L\,):{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

A machine state
(\,S_{A}\,,\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,,\,K\,)
makes
progress
unless
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is a pop
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
S_{a}
is empty. Note that types rule out the latter configuration, and thus ensure progress. Since types are preserved by the above theorem, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.5 (Machine progress).

Any state on a run from a typed state makes progress.

We define a typed notion of machine equivalence, where we expect input stacks to respect typing.

Definition 5.6 (Typed machine equivalence).

Typed machine equivalence
is defined as the relation
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

on similarly typed terms, which holds if:

\forall S_{A}:{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}.~\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor=\lfloor S_{A}\,{\Downarrow}\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rfloor~.

We can strengthen the
\eta
-axiom for the
typed
equational theory, as it ensures that the stack involved is non-empty.

Definition 5.7 (Typed equational theory).

The
typed equational theory

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
relates similarly typed terms of the RMC by the least congruence generated by the axioms of the untyped equational theory, together with the strengthened
\eta
-axiom

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=_{\eta}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}1_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}+m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}+1_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~.

The
\eta
-law of the
\kappa
-calculus is decomposed as follows, where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\not\in\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
, and indeed our symmetric
\eta
-axiom is equivalent.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=_{\nu}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x])\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=_{\eta}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~=~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Theorem 5.8 (Soundness of typed equational theory).

The typed equational theory is sound for typed machine equivalence:

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sim{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n_{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~.

6.
Categorical semantics

We give a sound and complete categorical semantics for the sum-free, star-free and function-symbol-free fragment of the RMC, illustrating how the core calculus provides a term language and operational interpretation of Frobenius monoids, and thus of a natural class of string diagrams.
Following this, we show that the corresponding fragments with linear and "Cartesian" variable policies provide a term language for symmetric monoidal and Cartesian categories, respectively.
The extension of the categorical semantics to include non-deterministic sum follows easily, but we leave to future work the categorical interpretation of iteration and of algebraic terms.

Frobenius monoids are used as a categorical abstraction for modelling a range of computational phenomena, many of interest to programming language theorists. While the literature contains computational interpretations of compact closed categories
(
18
)
and certain accounts of operational semantics of string diagrams
(
12
,
11
)
, to the best knowledge of the authors, the RMC gives the first syntactic account of Frobenius monoids via a notion of
\beta
-reduction, and the first operational account of Frobenius monoids via a stack machine.
To highlight the correspondence with string diagrams, we work with a single location, but the results extend easily to multiple locations
(
3
)
.

Notation

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
we denote by
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
the corresponding homset. We write the identity morphism on
X
as
\textsf{id}_{X}
, or simply
X
. Composition is written in diagrammatic order, using infix
(;\!)
. We denote the tensor product of a
symmetric monoidal category (SMC)

\mathcal{C}
by
\otimes
, its unit by
I
and its symmetry natural transformation as
sym

(
55
)
. Components of natural transformations are indexed by subscripts, which we sometimes omit. We elide all associativity and unit isomorphisms associated with monoidal categories and sometimes. Recall that a commutative monoid in a given SMC is an object
X
together with a multiplication
\mu:X\otimes X\to X
and a unit
\eta:I\to X
satisfying the expected associativity, commutativity and unit equations, and a cocommutative comonoid is defined dually. We denote by
CMon
the category of commutative monoids and monoid homomorphisms. We will use string diagrams throughout this section to describe (co-)monoids.

Definition 6.1 (Frobenius monoid).

In an SMC
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,1)
, an
extra-special commutative Frobenius monoid

(X,\delta,\epsilon,\mu,\eta)
consists of a commutative monoid
(X,\mu,\eta)
and a cocommutative comonoid (
X,\delta,\epsilon)
that satisfy the
Frobenius
,
special
, and
extra
equations, given below in the string-diagrammatic language of Figure
4
.

=
=
=
\textsf{id}_{I}
=

\mu:2\to 1
\eta:0\to 1
\epsilon:1\to 0
\delta:1\to 2

{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Figure 4.
Equipment of the Relational Machine Category: string diagrams, categorical combinators, and RMC-terms.
Equipment of the Relational Machine Category: string diagrams, categorical combinators, and RMC-terms.

The categorical abstraction of relations which is modelled by the RMC is that of
extra-hypergraph categories
, as defined below. This is a slight variant of the usual definition of
hypergraph category

(
32
)
, which additionally requires that the relevant Frobenius structures satisfy the
extra
equation. Indeed, a paradigmatic example of such a category is that of sets and relations.

Definition 6.2.

[Extra-hypergraph category]
An
extra-hypergraph category
is an SMC
\mathcal{C}
in which each object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is equipped with an extra-special commutative Frobenius structure
(\delta_{X},\epsilon_{X},\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
, which together satisfy the following coherences:

\displaystyle\delta_{X\otimes Y}

\displaystyle=(\delta_{X}\otimes\delta_{Y});(X\otimes\textsf{sym}_{X,Y}\otimes Y)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{X\otimes Y}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{X}\otimes\epsilon_{Y}

\displaystyle\mu_{X\otimes Y}

\displaystyle=(X\otimes\textsf{sym}_{X,Y}\otimes Y);(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{Y})

\displaystyle\eta_{X\otimes Y}

\displaystyle=\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}

and the unit coherence that
\eta_{I}=\textsf{id}_{I}=\epsilon_{I}
.
A functor of extra-hypergraph categories is a strong symmetric monoidal functor that additionally preserves the Frobenius structures.

We can define the hypergraph categorical structure of the RMC by assigning terms to string diagrams as in Figure
4
. We take objects to be the natural numbers, and wires to represent the input and output stacks, with the head of the stack at the top.
In contrast to the
\lambda
-calculus, categorical composition of terms is given by sequential compostion
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
rather than by substitution.
Right-action
k\otimes{M}
of the tensor product is given by stack expansion (as described in Lemma
5.3
).
Left-action
{M}\otimes k
lifts the
k
arguments from the stack as variables
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, to restore them after evaluating
{M}
on the remaining stack. The remaining equipment is familiar from the discussion in the introduction of this paper.

Definition 6.3.

The
Relational Machine Category
,
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]
, of RMC-terms, is defined by the following data.

•

Objects: natural numbers;

•

Morphisms:
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]({\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
is the set of closed simply-typed terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
over
\Sigma
, modulo
(=)
;

•

Identity: at object
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, the term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
;

•

Composition: for terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, their composition is
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
;

•

Extra-hypergraph structure: the tensor
\otimes
acts on objects
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\otimes{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}={\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m+n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, with unit
I={\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
; the right- and left-action of
\otimes
on morphisms, the symmetry, and the Frobenius monoids on object
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are given by Figure
4
; associators and unitors are given by the identity.

Note that it suffices to define a Frobenius monoid on the generating object
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, with Frobenius monoids at
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
defined using the compatibility conditions of Definition
6.2
.

Theorem 6.4 (Soundness).

The Relational Machine Category

\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]
is an extra-hypergraph category.

It is immediate from the axioms of Kleene algebra that
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]
is also
CMon
-enriched
,
i.e.
, every homset
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]({\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
is a commutative monoid with addition
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and unit
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, such that composition of morphisms distributes over addition. This is an easy and appropriate extension of the model to incorporate non-determinism.

Completeness of the categorical semantics for the core fragment follows an easy normal form argument.
Let
\emptyset
denote the empty signature.
Here, and below, let the free categories in question be generated over a single object (and the empty set of morphisms) unless otherwise specified.

Theorem 6.5 (Hypergraph completeness).

The subcategory of sum-free, star-free terms of
\textsf{RMC}[\emptyset]
is equivalent to the free extra-hypergraph category.

By restricting the variable policy of this fragment appropriately, we similarly recover term languages for symmetric monoidal and Cartesian categories. Let
linear
RMC-terms to be those for which each occurring variable appears exactly once in a
push
and once in a
pop
to its left. Then, since linear RMC-terms containing no function symbols are exactly permutations, we have the following.

Theorem 6.6 (Linear completeness).

The subcategory of linear, sum-free, star-free terms of
\textsf{RMC}[\emptyset]
is equivalent to the free symetric monoidal category.

We similarly take the
Cartesian
RMC-terms to be those for which each bound variable occurs exactly once in a
pop
, possibly in many
pushes
to its right, and in which no
pop
contains anything other than a variable.
Observe further that an RMC-signature
\Sigma
is just an algebraic signature with no equations, which can be used to generate a free Cartesian category as its Lawvere theory. Then a simple normal form argument proves the following.

Theorem 6.7 (Cartesian completeness).

The subcategory of sum-free, star-free terms of
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]
is equivalent to the free Cartesian category generated over
\Sigma
.

Consideration of the case where the signature includes an equational theory is left for future work: although it is unproblematic in the Cartesian case, the general case would require working with unification
modulo a theory
.

We conclude this section by giving some simple examples of RMC-terms and their corresponding string diagrams.
Following existing work on string diagrams for (the positive fragment of) Tarski’s calculus of relations
(
75
,
13
,
6
,
38
,
10
)
, we give terms and diagrams whose anticipated relational denotation is given by
top
(
i.e.
, everything is related),
intersection
, and
converse
. The remaining operations of the calculus of relations are already included in the grammar of regular expressions (see subsection
3.1
).
Top (
\top
) and intersection (
\cap
) are encoded as below.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists y.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[y]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists y.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,E\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,[x]\,{;}\,F\,{;}\,\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,[y]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}E\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
\top
E\cap F

We can further characterize the converse of a typed RMC-term as follows, first noting that
the compositions
\eta;\delta
and
\mu;\epsilon
form the cups and caps of a (self-dual) compact closed structure. By
\beta
-reduction we have that
\eta;\delta={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,[\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and dually, as shown below.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}0{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}2{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Relational converse can now be internalized in the following way.

Proposition 6.8 (Typed Duality).

For closed
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
we have
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}^{\dagger}~=~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\kern 1.0pt\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{y}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,[\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{y}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\,{;}\,M\,{;}\,\langle\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\rangle\,{;}\,[\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{y}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, where
|{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|=n
,
|{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{y}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|=m
.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{\dagger}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
=

7.
Relational Semantics

In this section, we investigate in detail the relational semantics of the typed RMC.
Although the denotational semantics is very close to the input/output behaviour of the big-step semantics, there are some differences: forgetting the output substitution, and the interpretation of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The former is interpreted semantically as the union of all possible interpretations of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as some closed term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, leaving the latter to become a simple equality check predicate; there is no need for unification since free variables are dealt with by (possibly infinite) non-determinism of the interpretation of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

For simplicity, we again work with a single location, but the results generalize easily. Now, let us recall the extra-hypergraph structure of the category of sets and relations.

Definition 7.1.

The category
Rel
of sets and relations forms an extra-hypergraph category where the tensor product is given by the Cartesian product, the unit given by the singleton set
\{\varepsilon\}
, and a Frobenius monoid on each object
A
given by
\delta_{A}=\{(x,(x,x))~|~x\in A\}
,
\epsilon_{A}=\{(x,\varepsilon)~|~x\in A\}
and with
\mu_{A}
and
\eta_{A}
by their respective converses.

The relational semantics of typed RMC-terms can easily be read off their normal forms: for example, compare the relational semantics of
\delta,\epsilon,\mu
and
\eta
with the RMC-terms interpreting the same morphisms in Figure
4
. Confirming this, we define an extra-hypergraph functor
\llbracket-\rrbracket:\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]\to\textsf{Rel}
. Let
T0
be the set of
closed
algebraic terms built over
\Sigma
,
i.e.
containing no variables.
To interpret open terms, we define a valuation
v:\textsf{Var}\to T0
as a partial function on the set of variables
Var
; the valuation
v\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leftarrow{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}
assigns
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and otherwise behaves as
v
. We elide the isomorphism between closed algebraic terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in the syntax of RMC-terms and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in T0
.
We extend the action of
v
to algebraic terms with variables
\Gamma
so that
v({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8},\ldots,t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}f(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}v({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}){\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}v({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}){\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
In the following, we consider relations
A\to B
as functions
A\to\mathcal{P}(B)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is the powerset functor, and we will write
f^{n}:X\to X
for the n-fold composition of a function
f:X\to X
and
X^{n}
for the n-fold product of a set
X
.

Definition 7.2 (Relational semantics).

The
relational semantics
of the typed RMC is defined inductively on types by
\llbracket{\vphantom{)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket=T0^{n}
.
We define the relation
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}
inductively on the type derivation of terms as follows, and may abbreviate this as
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}
.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=[S]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}k{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=[U~|~T\in\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S),~U\in\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(T)]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=[\,]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M+N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)\sqcup\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[t]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n+1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=[S\,v({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle t\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n+1{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}u\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[S]&\text{if}~v({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}u\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
[]&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in T0}\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leftarrow{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}}(S)

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}n{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}(S)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}^{n}(S)

Given a closed term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, its relational semantics
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket
(written without a subscript) is given by
\lfloor\,\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{\varepsilon}\rfloor
, where
\varepsilon
is the empty valuation.

From the following lemma, soundness of the semantics follows.

Lemma 7.3 (Substitution).

For any
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v\{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leftarrow{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}}=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{t/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}m{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}\kern 1.0pt}n\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket_{v}~.

Proposition 7.4 (Hypergraph functor).

The relational semantics
\llbracket-\rrbracket:\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]\to\textsf{Rel}
is a well-defined extra-hypergraph functor.

In fact, if we additionally consider
\textsf{RMC}[\Sigma]
and
Rel
as
CMon
-enriched categories, with the monoid on homsets in
Rel
given by the union of relations, it is easy to see the relational semantics functor is also
CMon
-enriched functor.

8.
Encoding computational models, II

We conclude with further encodings of computational models, demonstrating the use of locations, an innovation of the Functional Machine Calculus
(
42
,
4
)
that may capture stateful behaviour.

8.1.
Guarded Command Language

In 1975 Dijkstra introduced the
Guarded Command Language (GCL)
: a simple non-deterministic imperative programming language
(
28
)
.

The eponymous
guarded command

B\to S
is given by a sequence of statements
S
to be evaluated when the
guard
Boolean expression
B
is true. Statements are atomic actions — here, we consider writing to a memory cell (
a:=N
) — or are sets of guarded commands wrapped in
conditional
(
if
) or
iteration
(
do
) keywords. The former construct executes (non-deterministically) one of the statements whose guard is true, and aborts execution if none are. The iteration construct successively executes (non-deterministically) one of the statements whose guard is true, and terminates successfully when none are. Reading from a memory cell
a
is denoted
!a
.
The full grammar of the GCL is given below:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{~}l@{}}\text{Boolean Expressions:}&B&\Coloneqq\textsf{tt}\mid\textsf{ff}\mid\ldots\\
\text{Numeric Expressions:}&N&\Coloneqq n\mid\,!a\mid\ldots\\
\text{Guarded Commands:}&C&\Coloneqq\textsf{abort}\mid C_{1}\square\,C_{2}\mid B\rightarrow S\\
\text{Statements:}&S&\Coloneqq\textsf{skip}\mid S_{1};S_{2}\mid\textsf{if}~C\mid\textsf{do}~C\mid a\coloneqq N\end{array}

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a
ranges over a set of variable names. ​
3
3

3

Note, we make several simplifications with regards to the original grammar. In particular, guarded commands and lists of statements are allowed to be empty. We consider only a meagre set of expressions, but, with a little more work,
e.g.
operators such as addition or comparison may be accounted for.

Input and output is performed by manipulation of memory cells, which we take to hold integers — so, in standard imperative style, programs are called for their side-effects.

The embedding of the GCL in the RMC is given below.

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{tt}\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\top]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket!a\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]a\,{;}\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{ff}\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\bot]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket a\coloneqq N\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket N\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\exists xy.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,\langle y\rangle\,{;}\,[y]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket n\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[n]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket B\rightarrow S\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket B\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\langle\top\rangle\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\llbracket S\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{abort}\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{skip}\rrbracket

\displaystyle={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\llbracket S_{1};S_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{if}~C\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket C\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\textsf{do}~C\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket C\rrbracket{\vphantom{(}{}^{*}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Encodings of global memory cells are familiar from the FMC
(
42
)
: locations model mutable variables, whose value is held as the single item on the corresponding stack. The result of an expression, including reading from a memory cell
(!a)
, is pushed to the main (unnamed) stack; updating a memory cell
(a:=N)
replaces the value stored at
a
with the value pushed to the main stack by
\llbracket N\rrbracket
.

Proposition 8.1 (The RMC implements GCL).

If the GCL successfully executes a guarded command
C
on input memory
S_{A}
, with output memory
T_{A}
, then there exists an RMC run

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llbracket\mathcal{C}\rrbracket}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\end{array}~.

Note that our encoding in fact outputs (non-deterministically) the memory at every stage of the computation, and not only the memories at the end of a successful GCL execution.

8.2.
Turing Machines

We demonstrate an encoding of a Turing machine
\mathcal{M}
with a set of
states

S
, initial state
{i}\in S
, halting state
{h}\in S
, alphabet
\Sigma
, blank symbol
{0}\in\Sigma
, and transition function
\delta:(S\setminus\{{h}\})\times\Sigma\to S\times\Sigma\times\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
.
To do so, we work with an RMC signature consisting of
S\cup\Sigma
and three locations
l,r,
and
q
, used to record the tape to the left of the head, the tape under and to the right of the head (in reverse), and the current state, respectively.

A Turing machine
\mathcal{M}
then encodes as follows. Below left are the encodings of tape moves
m\in\{\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{R}\}
and of a transition in
\delta
as a five-tuple
d=(s,a,s^{\prime},a^{\prime},m)
. Then
\mathcal{M}
encodes as an iterated non-deterministic sum of transitions, below right.

\begin{aligned} \llbracket\boldsymbol{L}\rrbracket&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}l\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\llbracket\boldsymbol{R}\rrbracket&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists x.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}r\langle x\rangle\,{;}\,[x]l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\llbracket d\rrbracket&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}q\langle s\rangle\,{;}\,r\langle a\rangle\,{;}\,[s^{\prime}]q\,{;}\,[a^{\prime}]r\,{;}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\llbracket m\rrbracket\end{aligned}\qquad\llbracket\mathcal{M}\rrbracket={\left(\sum_{d\in\delta}\llbracket d\rrbracket\right)^{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\ast\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

Instantiating the tape in both directions
S_{l}
and
S_{r}
with streams of blank symbols
4
4

4

It also easy enough to simulate an infinite tape with stacks, using special end-of-stack markers
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\bot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\top\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The encoding of a read of
a\in\Sigma
then additionally checks for the end of either stack, pushing markers along as needed: that is, in the case of
a=0
, using
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}r\langle a\rangle+r\langle\bot\rangle\,{;}\,[\bot]l+r\langle\top\rangle\,{;}\,[\top]r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
instead of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}r\langle a\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
, we have the following result.

Proposition 8.2 (The RMC implements Turing machines).

A Turing machine
\mathcal{M}
halts with tape
T_{l}
and
T_{r}
to the left and right of the head, respectively, if and only if there exists a machine run

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{l}\cdot S_{r}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}s\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{q}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llbracket\mathcal{M}\rrbracket}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{l}\cdot T_{r}\cdot\,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}h\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{q}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\end{array}~.

8.3.
Interaction Nets

Interaction nets are a graphical model of computation first introduced as a generalisation of multiplicative proof nets
(
52
)
. A term calculus for interaction nets
(
30
)
is given as follows. A
net
is a pair
\langle T\mid\Delta\rangle
where
T
is a stack of algebraic terms over a signature
\Sigma
, and
\Delta=\{t_{1}\doteq u_{1},\dots,t_{n}\doteq u_{n}\}
is a set of formal equations. Each variable occurs at most twice in a net. Rewriting of nets is performed according to a set of rules
\mathcal{R}
, with each rule a pair of terms
f(S)\bowtie g(U)
such that
f\neq g
and with each variable occurring exactly twice. The set
\mathcal{R}
is further required to be
deterministic
: there is at most one rule for each pair of
f
and
g
; and
symmetric
: if
f(S)\bowtie g(U)
then
g(U)\bowtie f(S)
. Variables are
local
to each rule, and are instantiated with fresh variables during computation.

The evaluation relation
(\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.75 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.92 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\mathcal{R}})
is generated by the following rules, adapted from
(
30
)
. We write
T\doteq U
for the pairwise equations of
T
and
U
of equal length, and
\varepsilon
the empty set of equations.

\displaystyle\langle T\mid\Delta,x\doteq u\rangle

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.75 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.92 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\mathcal{R}}~\langle\{u/x\}T\mid\{u/x\}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle\langle T\mid\Delta,u\doteq x\rangle

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

To encode interaction nets we use two locations
l,r
to hold the left and right sides of each equation in
\Delta=\{t_{1}\doteq u_{1},\dots,t_{n}\doteq u_{n}\}
, which is then interpreted as the memory below left. Below it is the interpretation of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
, where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are the variables occurring in
S
and
U
. A set of rules
\mathcal{R}
is encoded as below right. The terms
T
in a configuration
\langle T\mid\Delta\rangle
are held on the unnamed main location.

\begin{aligned} \llbracket\{t_{i}\doteq u_{1}\}_{i\leq n}\rrbracket&=({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\dots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}t_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})_{l}\cdot({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}u_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\dots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}u_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})_{r}\\
\llbracket f(S)\bowtie g(U)\rrbracket&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\exists\accentset{\rightharpoonup}{x}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}l\langle f(S)\rangle\,{;}\,r\langle g(U)\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{aligned}\quad\llbracket\mathcal{R}\rrbracket=\left(\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\llbracket r\rrbracket\right)^{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\ast\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Proposition 8.3 (Interaction nets embed).

For a set of rules
\mathcal{R}
and a configuration
\langle T\mid\Delta\rangle
we have that
\langle T\mid\Delta\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to8pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.5pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{

if and only if

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~,~}r@{~)}}T\cdot\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llbracket{\mathcal{R}}\rrbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon\\
